brings all the infinite world realms
under its power in an instant, in a moment;
there is nothing at all that does not come under its power.
hrīḥ"

From the Net of Illusion, having brought all without exception under power, this is the fifty-seventh chapter, on the homa of subjugation.

Then the Tathāgata, the king of the wrathful ones called Radiating, drew these maṇḍalas forth from his own expanse and spoke this inspired utterance:

"The maṇḍala of great bliss blazes,
churning with many radiating rays of light.
Meditate on it as blazing supremely as the field of merit
and filling the sky of the ten directions.
It is encircled by vajra jewels;
the qualities of the Victors are equal to space.
trāṃ In the four directions and the center, the light of primordial wisdom blazes;
the maṇḍala is encircled by jeweled nooses.
Furthermore, various mudrās,
and all the wrathful ones as well, radiate joy;
great white light blazes,
and the various mudrās transform into anything at all.
It is encircled by gods and goddesses.
With various requisites of offering,
white and fragrant woods,
and all the other offerings,
make abundant offerings to the maṇḍala.
Having yourself blazed intensely as the jewel,
trāṃ the white syllable blazes as light.
All the wrathful ones are likewise.
In the maṇḍala that has become manifest,
with pā śa hoḥ and jaḥ hūṃ
summon all of the five paths in the ten directions,
and meditate on light radiating as they dissolve into the expanse.
All is equal to the buddha fields;
it does not exist from anywhere else:
it is the stable primordial wisdom of illusion.
Furthermore, in the inconceivable maṇḍalas,
the vajra blazes in the expanse of jewels,
and the maṇḍala is encircled all around with ornaments.
Furthermore, gods and goddesses
make offerings pervading the ten directions.
The qualities are equal to the fields of the Victors.
trāṃ Make offerings with songs and dances.
Having made abundant offerings filling the ten directions,
all the buddha fields without exception,
with jeweled offerings that increase glory,
one revels abundantly in the vajra expanse.
hoḥ trāṃ Vajra pride with its retinue
makes all the infinite world realms
increase in an instant, in a moment;
there is nothing at all that has not increased."

From the Net of Illusion, this is the fifty-eighth chapter, on the homa of increase by the King Who Increases.

Then the Tathāgata Vairocana, the great wrathful one, transformed all the maṇḍalas into a great blaze and spoke this inspired utterance:

"The maṇḍala of primordial wisdom has no center and no edge;
it is inconceivable and spontaneously present.
All the maṇḍalas of the Victors without exception
arise without clinging in the Sugata himself.
oṃ From a circular, blazing maṇḍala
encircled all around by blazing wheels,
light blazes intensely in yellow.
The wondrous body and the rest
should be meditated upon by the great being;
all the maṇḍalas will be accomplished.
The seed syllable is a yellow oṃ.
Inviting the fire god as before,
all the wrathful maṇḍalas without exception
are the vajra of delusion,
radiating various rays of light,
with blazing wheels and mudrās,
adorned by hosts of gods and goddesses.
Seats, abodes and the expanse itself:
in those maṇḍalas of the Victors,
in those that abide in the ten directions,
having summoned with the cakra and the hook,
and dissolved them into the expanse of the maṇḍala,
the fierce, the turbulent, the malevolent and the wicked
become peaceful, vivid and radiant.
oṃ Furthermore, in the inconceivable maṇḍalas,
of the maṇḍala of blazing wheels,
encircled by oṃ and by lions' heads,
all the vajra mudrās that adorn it
should be encircled by the peaceful ones of the age of perfection.
The king with a beautiful body, the blazing wheel,
the ornaments peaceful and white,
and supreme awakening blazing, are meditated on as vivid.
They move through the sky and float through the air.
Furthermore, all six classes of beings without exception
will become peaceful; of this there is no doubt.
Having made offerings with dance and song,
all the malevolent ones of the ten directions without exception
will completely cast off their malevolent minds
and turn into the light of the white deity.
Blissful awakening radiates and blazes with light.
Vajra delusion with its retinue
pacifies all the infinite world realms
in an instant, in a moment;
there is nothing at all that has not been pacified."

From the Net of Illusion, all without exception having been pacified, this is the fifty-ninth chapter, on the homa of pacification.

Then the Tathāgata, the Blessed One, Great Delight, entered into equipoise in the meditative absorption called the Blessing of the Spontaneously Present Samaya and spoke this inspired utterance:

"To the supreme maṇḍala of the assembly of e,
blazing, greatly fierce and ferocious,
make the offering with a wrathful mind;
even the merit body of the buddhas is destroyed.
On the five nectars or the five foods,
make the effigy together with the name.
Having summoned it, strike it with the vajra kīla;
having reduced it to dust, offer it to the assembly.
In the maṇḍala of the assembly of e,
when they are linked and bound in a chain,
they are bound; if it is moved, the ten directions shake;
intoxicated, they may even be burned.
To the supreme maṇḍala of the assembly of vaṃ,
the great mountain of blazing splendor,
make the offering with a mind of passion;
even vajra speech comes under one's power.
On the five nectars or the five foods,
make the effigy together with the name.
Strike it with the kīla of vajra passion,
and offer it, dissolving it into the assembly of passion.
In the maṇḍala of the assembly of vaṃ,
when they are linked and bound in a chain,
they are summoned, and whatever it is follows behind;
whatever one desires will come accordingly.
To the supreme maṇḍala of the assembly of ma,
of great splendor, blazing, gathering and radiating,
make the offering with a joyful mind;
its qualities are equal to the bounds of space.
On the five nectars or the five foods,
visualize the qualities as a wish-fulfilling treasure.
Strike with the kīla of pride radiating joy,
and offer it so that vajra splendor increases.
In the maṇḍala of the assembly of ma,
when they are linked and bound in a chain,
a great magical display of blazing splendor unfolds,
and the wish-fulfilling filling the sky comes about.
To the supreme maṇḍala of the assembly of ya,
shining with clear splendor,
make the offering with a clear mind;
even the greatly fierce and ferocious become calm.
On the five nectars or the five foods,
visualize the nature of the fierce, ferocious and turbulent.
Strike with the kīla of calm clarity,
and offer it to the assembly of blazing calm.
In the maṇḍala of the assembly of ya,
when they are linked and bound in a chain,
they are blessed as blazing calm,
and everything becomes unmoving.
To the hosts of attendants, maidservants and slave women,
give the remainder offering they desire.
Having loudly proclaimed their samaya,
meditate that they perform whatever action is desired.
In former times the great glorious Heruka,
who tamed all without exception,
those extremely malevolent and of fierce disposition,
had wondrous power and blessing.
Having brought the Great God (Śiva) and the rest under his power,
he made the retinue of Īśvara his attendants,
and appointed each to its own tasks.
As they have promised and vowed,
bring these about without obstruction and swiftly.
If you do not act as you vowed,
as you yourselves swore an oath,
do not transgress that commitment.
If you transgress that samaya,
your heads, bodies and hearts too will be cut
into seven pieces by the wrathful yakṣa.
Therefore, do as you are charged
by the yogin mantra holder.
If you do not bring about that action,
then, just as you yourselves swore and promised,
you will rot, decay and burn, and go to hell.
Fully perform the actions
of the yogin who abides by the samaya,
and protect his retinue as an only child.
The sorceresses, too, will obtain accomplishment.
Bring about the actions you are charged with!"

Thus he commanded. This is the principal action for accomplishing the wrathful ones.

"Through the great dances and songs
of the great Heruka and the rest,
everything at all is done.
Through the great dances and songs
of the great Treasure Holder and the rest,
if gathering is wanted, they gather;
if increase is wanted, they increase.
Through the great dances and songs
of the great Swollen One and the rest,
if calm is wanted, they dissolve;
if stupor is wanted, they intoxicate.
Through the great dances and songs
of the great Lion-Faced One and the rest,
everything is devoured entirely;
not even a particle of dust is left.
Through the great dances and songs
of the great Laughing-Faced One and the rest,
everything is devoured entirely,
and all are utterly ruined.
Through the great dances and songs
of the attendants, maidservants and slave women,
things are dispatched and sent off:
everything at all is done.
In trees, thickets of saplings,
a lone tree, or a place touched by fire,
with clarity, joy, passion
and wrath, perform the kali intensely."

When this was spoken, the Tathāgatas themselves were purified within the Tathāgatas themselves.

From The Secret Essence, Ascertaining Reality, this is the sixtieth chapter, called the Blessing of Spontaneously Present Enlightened Activity.

Then, from the King of Tantras of the Tathāgatas, the Net of Illusion, it was taught that all phenomena are gathered into mind and phenomena; that all wisdom is gathered into the four mothers, and the four mothers too are gathered into one; and that all skillful means are gathered into the four families, and the four families too are gathered into one. Having taught that everything is gathered into the expanse and the primordial wisdom of the expanse, he summarized the meaning of the words from beginning to end and spoke again as follows. This is the supreme root of the meaning:

"All without exception, with nothing left out,
is unapprehended and unattached: the nature of reality.
Likewise the expanse itself appears as many and various skillful means;
skillful means itself is an illusion, a mirage, the moon in water, a reflection.
That itself is self-aware primordial wisdom, appearing variously without attachment.
Because, endowed with skillful means and wisdom, one has arrived at the supreme state,
however one acts, it is inexhaustible, nondual, and does not become an obscuration.
It is primordially the play of the supreme, of wisdom and great skillful means.
That itself is the various mudrās, the emanations, self-awareness.
Because cause and result are primordially inseparable, neither earlier nor later,
the result becomes the path, and they are joined equally at a single time.
This is the source from which all wish-fulfilling jewels arise,
the treasure in which all is gathered, the wish-fulfilling treasury,
free of duality, the very expanse of all, equal and unequal.
I and no-self, the various signs, are inseparable;
empty and not empty, not abiding, are gathered in suchness.
The self-arisen vajra maṇḍala sends forth inexhaustible maṇḍalas.
The maṇḍalas of the passed beyond and the not passed beyond, inconceivable and spontaneously present,
endowed with the inexhaustible, perfect the six wheels of ornament;
the supreme garland of self-arisen luminosity of the unborn expanse,
primordially uncontrived and unmade, is clear in the state of self-awareness.
All arising and entering is gathered in the state of the great treasure of self-awareness.
The ten directions without exception, with nothing left out, all gathered together,
and all the inconceivable celestial palaces of the realms, supreme great jewels,
are rightly enclosed in the two, the unborn expanse and one's own awareness.
Aspirational conduct, emptiness, cause and result, are purified again and again, unapprehended.
The abode of the Jewel, primordial luminosity, without attachment and unattached:
mere names, the supreme play, perfected by supreme skillful means and wisdom.
On the very steps of the path, the ten perfections are completed and cause and result are practiced.
Truly endowed with the vajra of equality, the great skillful means,
it is spoken of as without going, without coming, not abiding, all-pervading and without pervasion.
a ho"

From the King of Tantras, the Net of Illusion, this is the sixty-first chapter, teaching that the perfections, the twelve grounds, the supreme through skillful means, and all the buddha fields without exception are gathered into mind and phenomena.

Then the Tathāgata, abiding in equipoise in the maṇḍala of the five primordial wisdoms, spoke this inspired utterance:

"All the five aggregates and the limbs as well
are awakening, abiding as the five syllables.
Linked as oṃ and mūṃ,
they arise from the very vajra of awakening.
In general, all that is expressed,
all of it, is mere name, the letter a.
Arising and perishing themselves are the five letters.
That is the mind of awakening,
from which unapprehending supreme awakening arises.
Three dawning, five blazing, ten in abundance:
it is the inexhaustible treasure of jewels,
endowed with the six higher perceptions and adorned with the excellent marks.
Radiating from e and gathering into a,
it is the very expanse of the secret bindu.
Clouds arise from the expanse of the sky,
pervading all, a variety of clouds.
From within the forty-five, inconceivable,
the blessing is marvelous and most wondrous;
it has become a treasure of inexhaustible qualities.
ho Primordially, equality is the expanse of the secret maṇḍala;
not other than the expanse, it is primordially the state of the bindu.
The expanse of primordial wisdom itself is in the manner of a reflection, an illusion;
in the very act of appearing, it abides without agent or action.
The meaning of the forty-five great magical displays of primordial wisdom,
the inconceivable, spontaneously present, sublime maṇḍalas,
abides in equality, skillful means and wisdom being nondual.
Meditating with realization of the meaning of the three kinds of purity and equality,
it is the supreme state, the realization of the meaning through the four kinds of equality.
Cause and result, spontaneously present, are gathered into the four perfections.
The five primordial wisdoms, complete, are gathered in body, speech and mind.
From the peaceful path without attachment, one enters the bindu.
The five primordial wisdoms, complete, do not pass beyond their sphere of activity,
and are fully endowed with the unattached maṇḍala of luminosity.
By the king, self-awareness, all without exception
in the pure and equal maṇḍala are joined as wrathful gods and goddesses.
Phenomena are empty by their own nature.
The various conceptual thoughts that grasp
the inner and the outer of the inner
are the momentary expanse without entities.
That itself is the path of complete purity.
The inverted itself is the genuine;
ignorance is clear primordial wisdom;
affliction and suffering are supreme awakening;
all is the abode of the supreme qualities.
a ho All the maṇḍalas of the Victors of the ten directions without exception,
unraveling the definitive meaning, are gathered into the forty-five.
Their condensed meaning is gathered into five letters;
the meaning of the five is gathered within three letters;
those three are gathered into the letter a,
and a itself, unborn, dissolves into the expanse of perfect equality.
a ho Unapprehended emptiness cannot be expressed;
the unapprehended Tathāgatas, too, are inconceivable;
the supreme unborn expanse, too, is inconceivable;
the unceasing mind of awakening is without arising or perishing.
The blessing of compassion,
without going, without coming, not abiding, radiates."

Thus he spoke to himself.

"From the expanse the expanse arises, clear as the mudrā of skillful means;
from skillful means the expanse arises, clear as the mudrā of the mother.
Because primordial wisdom is abundant in the expanse through the expanse,
the maṇḍalas of the assemblies of the mind of awakening cannot be expressed."

This is the sixty-second chapter, on the dissolution of all the definitive meaning of the maṇḍala of the Tathāgatas, without exception, into the mudrā without characteristics.

Then the Tathāgata, having emanated as the King Amitāyus, spoke this inspired utterance:

Condensing the teaching of the Net of Illusion:
In the maṇḍala of bhruṃ and viśva,
upon the maṇḍala of ma and a,
having thoroughly meditated on the vajra wheel,
meditate on the meaning of the forty-five,
and meditate that by it the ten directions are filled.
Summon with bhruṃ and jaḥ hūṃ vaṃ;
the supreme essence of body, speech, and mind
should be dissolved into body, speech, and mind.
Take the nṛ itself either as actual or as mentally imagined.
The supreme vase, the excellent one,
is this very place where life is deposited.
Summon it again and again,
and when it has directly ripened,
conceal it once more within the expanse.
With the protection of the vajra armor
make it stable, and ward off with the wheel.
Dissolve it into the essence of five syllables.
All the buddhas, entirely without exception,
meditate on as King Amitāyus:
his body colored like a gleaming snow mountain,
adorned with various garlands of ornaments,
with a garland of wish-fulfilling jewels,
a casket of wish-fulfilling jewels,
jeweled ornaments and an excellent vase,
and other things that accomplish the benefit of beings,
inconceivable, limitless, and supreme.
If the obstructors will not give up the nṛ,
Amitāyus and the wrathful ones,
filling the realm of space in the ten directions,
uttering fierce sounds and cries,
should subdue them all without exception.
Confer Amitāyus as the empowerment.
Through the life approach and close approach,
the life accomplishment and great accomplishment,
through the richness of skillful means and wisdom,
whatever actions one wishes are accomplished.
The goddess of life empowerment is she who increases continuity,
white in color, blazing with radiating light;
her hand full of jewels,
she transforms her various mudrās into whatever is needed.
To all the six classes of beings without exception
who dwell in the ten directions of the six worlds,
she grants this beneficial life empowerment.
There is no increaser of continuity beyond this.
This great magical display, the recitation of syllables,
should be done without error in the threefold manner;
do not interrupt the rite of mantra recitation;
perform it with supreme familiarity, clarity, and stability.
oṃ oṃ oṃ oṃ oṃ hūṃ hūṃ hūṃ hūṃ hūṃ svāṃ svāṃ svāṃ svāṃ svāṃ āṃḥ āṃḥ āṃḥ āṃḥ āṃḥ hāṃ hāṃ hāṃ hāṃ hāṃ brūṃ brūṃ brūṃ brūṃ brūṃ bā bā bā bā bā jra jra jra jra jra ā ā ā ā ā yu yu yu yu yu śṣe śṣe śṣe śṣe śṣe hūṃ hūṃ hūṃ hūṃ hūṃ a a a a a o
If the lives have not come under control,
then the Sugatas too, as Amitāyus,
with forms of body, speech, and mind
fill the realm of space in the ten directions,
and with mudrās and various weapons
conquer the māras of the worlds in the ten directions,
and bring the lives of the five classes of beings under control.
With the plow mudrā and the like,
having stirred up the realms of the worlds below,
summon life back from the abode of Yama
and dissolve it into the five essences.
With the mudrā of the great wheel,
having stirred up the realms of the worlds above,
from Īśvara, the Great God (Śiva), and the others,
and from the obstructors moving through the intermediate space,
forcefully summon the life of the nṛ.
Having dissolved it into the syllables of the wheel,
then those five syllables
should be dissolved into the five places of the nṛ.
oṃ oṃ vajra kapaca vajra rakṣa hūṃ oṃ sarva tathāgata vajra adhitiṣṭha vajra svāhā
This is the rite of protection by mudrā.
The five syllables are a single syllable;
the single syllable, beyond objectification, dissolves into the expanse.
oṃ
The wish-fulfilling vase, a precious jewel,
adorned with ornaments and garlands of jewels,
with the five precious substances and the five medicines,
the five grains, the five incenses, and the five white mustard seeds:
the mantra practitioner, very steadfast and intelligent,
should recite and meditate on the mantra without impairing any of its limbs.
When it has been furnished with the five ornaments,
all the maṇḍalas will be accomplished.
Then that son of noble family,
having furnished all the requisites without exception,
pleasing and honoring with various kinds of offering water,
will obtain the accomplishments.

This is the sixty-third chapter, the supremely secret chapter on the sublime empowerment, the rite of life from the Net of Illusion.

Then the Tathāgata Vajra Gatherer, having again condensed it into what is called "Speaking So That the Five Classes of Beings Are Powerless," spoke this inspired utterance:

"The expanse of the secret maṇḍala itself,
within the state of Samantabhadra's compassion,
endowed with body, speech, and mind and perfect in qualities,
should be joined in the field of equality.
Through the vajra, utterly secret,
the vajra thoroughly joined,
meditate that it arises as oṃ and mūṃ;
meditate that all dissolves into the expanse.
In the sublime secret maṇḍala
and in those drops and mantra,
the excellent lines of nṛ, ash, and manta,
with excellent porticoes and enclosing gateways,
single-pointed vajras blazing with light,
and the sublime hook of summoning:
draw single-pointed and three-pointed vajras.
Furnish the maṇḍala with its materials,
and present various offering waters as offerings.
yaṃ
Light radiates from smoke-colored wisdom.
From a, upon a moon maṇḍala,
from jaḥ come countless hooks;
by them make it enter the heart.
From ma, upon a sun maṇḍala,
from jaḥ come many hooks;
from the heart of whoever is summoned
draw it out and place it in his mouth.
From ma, upon a sun maṇḍala,
from jaḥ come many hooks;
from the heart of whoever is summoned
draw it out and place it in his mouth.
This mantra should be fully recited:
oṃ jaḥ hūṃ vaṃ ho a nṛ pra du, then the name, pra veśa.
Recite it for whatever action is desired.
Interlace the fingers of both hands,
hold them tightly clenched as a fist,
and with the ring fingers, the little fingers, and the two thumbs,
draw the thumbs out in between.
oṃ, the name, pra veśa ā ā.
Strike forcefully with ash and white mustard seed,
and make that name enter within.
When it has descended, bind it with the noose of the vajra chain.
Ask, "What are you?"
Whatever it is, a god, a spirit, or the like:
if it is a god, make offerings to it;
if it is a graha, mix sandalwood and saffron,
give it that to drink, and fully recite this mantra:
oṃ vajra dhaka krodha samaya hūṃ phaṭ
If it will not speak,
interlace the fingers of both hands outward,
clench them as fists, and join the little fingers;
raise them upright and touch them to the mouth of the one into whom it has descended.
By reciting the mantra oṃ vajra bhāṣa vije pra veśa hūṃ hūṃ many times,
think that wisdom, unobstructed and free of obscuration,
enters into those words of truth.
At the tip of the tongue is a white a;
upon it a moon maṇḍala;
if one meditates on a red oṃ jaḥ dhīḥ there,
it will speak without doubt.
Meditating on oneself as ṭākki hūṃ,
whatever beings of the five paths there may be,
such as those of a and nṛ,
recite oṃ ṭākki hūṃ together with their own mantras
a hundred thousand times, and summon them.
Recite with pra veśa as well.
Further, with jaḥ hūṃ vaṃ hoḥste:
the support for placing and bringing it down
is a child twelve years old;
bring it down into one or into five.
A drop, a vase, a blazing mirror,
and all the other supports as well:
the rite of making it speak is complete.
With the vajra-claw mudrā
summon with all the summoning mudrās.
jaḥ hūṃ
Gather Īśvara, the Great God (Śiva), and the others,
and entrust them with tasks in the same way.
If they will not comply with ṭākki hūṃ jaḥs,
then with Hayagrīva, pressing down their abode and their land,
their borders, head and tail,
recite fiercely and summon with the hook.
Without mistaking the life-essence,
torment them and draw them forcefully.
Whatever means of summoning there may be,
transcendent or worldly,
summon without hesitation.
When the various tasks are completed,
into the five vases prepared before
put the five medicines, the five grains, the five precious substances,
the mother of the family, and Amṛtakuṇḍalin,
all the actions one wishes,
and whatever else is needed.
oṃ vajra amṛta a a a oṃ mahā vajra amṛte phaṭ
One also says ja gad ja gad ca.
Recite oṃ buddha locani svāhā.
In that sublime secret maṇḍala,
speak to the very perfection of the five wisdoms:
"Essence of wisdom, listen!
Again and again, vividly,
meditate always with supreme awakening."
The accomplishment of doing so is this:
transcendent and worldly
accomplishments are obtained through intelligible speech."

Thus it is, in vajra secret words. For the sake of beings it was uttered once again.

This is the sixty-fourth chapter, on bringing down the pra.

Then the glorious Śrī Heruka, having entered into nondual union with the consort Krodhīśvarī, emanated many maṇḍalas of wrathful ones from the vajra of the mind of awakening. He brought under his control, entirely without exception, the great worldly sovereign goddesses, the lords, the kings, the kings of kings, the ministers, the generals, inconceivable hundreds of thousands of retinues, and all the rest. As for the great terrifying goddesses, both now and in the time to come, he appointed some to the activity of increasing life; he appointed some to increase wealth; he appointed some to travel unobstructed through the great elements; he appointed some to travel with their forms invisible; he appointed some to transform into any of various forms; he appointed some to bring the hostile side under control; he appointed some to bring decline and bewilderment; he appointed some to wither and cast out. Having appointed them to whatever other various tasks were desired, he spoke this inspired utterance:

"Through the vajra that knows equality,
the supreme lords of the world are brought under control.
Through the blessing of great compassion,
the various aims of beings are shown.
Because one is endowed with skillful means and wisdom,
it is accomplished just as it is focused upon.
This is the self-arisen god of gods,
who sports endowed with love and compassion.
Through great compassion and skill in means,
though he engages with each and every one,
habitual tendencies do not accumulate,
and the accumulations of merit and wisdom are perfected.
Apart from the conduct of skillful means,
there is not even an atom of deluded thought of apprehended and apprehender.
Because he is inseparable from suchness,
he abides always in the luminosity of intrinsic nature."

Then one should meditate on oneself, as in the meaning of the tantra set out above, as whichever of those wrathful and peaceful deities one is accustomed to. When the samādhi has become workable and the deity has become clear, one performs the recitation of one's own deity, hundreds of thousands of times.

To summon the Great God (Śiva): the hook, the noose, spho ṭa vaṃ. mahā devā hūṃ jaḥ jaḥ hūṃ vaṃ hoḥ.
From the heart-place of the lord of gods
draw him forth again and again.
mama trag śa mahā deva gṛhṇa gṛhṇa hūṃ hūṃ
The god's bodily color is gleaming white;
his body is adorned with every jewel;
on his crown a wish-fulfilling jewel blazes;
and what he holds in his right hand
is a vase of the water of wish-fulfilling jewels.
His qualities, the five kinds of precious things,
fulfill the hopes of all the gods.
Dwelling with the queen Utmata,
he is always endowed with the five sense pleasures.
Oneself as Vairocana, and the celestial palace;
the form of the god in the manner described before.
Recite the god's seed syllable a hundred thousand times,
so that it is endowed with life.
From in front, draw him with jaḥ hūṃ vaṃ hoḥs;
from behind, the vajra holder pushes him.
Closer and closer, until there is no duality,
and when the Great God has become clear,
set him to do whatever action is desired.
The Great God and his whole retinue
will gather, accomplished and brought under control,
and will perform whatever actions are desired.

Then he spoke this rite for accomplishing the Four Great Kings.

Through the samādhi of the supreme mind of awakening,
all the four directions without exception,
for the accomplishment of the holder of vidyā-mantras,
and the Four Great Kings too are brought under control.
Meditate on oneself as the Heruka of the four families,
just as was set out above.
With the deity itself always clear,
meditate clearly on the form of the deity.
Oneself nondual with the deity,
meditate clearly in the true sense.
Then King Dhṛtarāṣṭra:
his form like a snow mountain at sunrise,
endowed with a hundred sacrifices and rays of light;
in his right hand a vajra trident,
in his left he holds a vase of nectar.
in tra sā ya svā hā jaḥ hūṃ vaṃ hoḥ tra mi ta hūṃ hūṃ su ra ta baghra jāḥ jā jā hūṃ hūṃ
Having recited this a hundred thousand times, oneself as Vajra Heruka,
with those mudrās of drawing in,
draw him from the heart of the lord of gods;
from behind, the vajra holder pushes him.
When he has come under one's power as lord of gods,
set him to do whatever action is desired.
He accomplishes each and every thing;
he gives forth to all and devours all.
All the retinues of the god without exception
truly gather,
and all follow like servants.
To accomplish King Virūḍhaka:
oneself as Ratna Heruka,
with body color as it occurs in the tantra,
and the mudrās likewise.
Through the blessing of light radiating and gathering,
Virūḍhaka is brought under control.
dhṛ ta dhṛ ta svā hā jaḥ hūṃ vaṃ hoḥ gṛhṇa gṛhṇa du rthid svā hā haṃ haṃ haṃ ratna jaḥ hūṃ vaṃ hoḥ
Having recited this essence a hundred thousand times,
practice the samādhi so that it is endowed with life.
Oneself as Ratna Heruka,
from Virūḍhaka's heart-place,
seizing him firmly with jaḥ,
one should summon him before oneself;
from behind, the vajra holder pushes him.
Then, when he has become directly present,
set him to do whatever action is desired.
His color blazes as the color of gold.
Gather all his retinues to oneself,
and the palace of wish-fulfilling jewels is accomplished.
To accomplish King Virūpākṣa:
oneself as Padma Heruka,
with body color as before,
and the manner of the mudrās likewise.
Having clearly recited the essence a hundred thousand times,
when the samādhi and the mudrā can be transformed,
and one has become clear as the deity:
that king's bodily color
is deep red and gleaming;
in his right hand he holds a vase of nectar,
in his left, in the manner of the five nectars;
he fulfills the hopes of the nāgas of the west,
and has a hood of nine snake heads.
To bring him fully under control:
bi rū ḍha ka svā hā jaḥ hūṃ vaṃ hoḥ nā ga śi ni bi ya phag śa phag jaḥ hūṃ vaṃ hoḥ
Having recited this many hundreds of thousands of times,
bring him under one's own control.
Having meditated clearly on oneself as the deity,
from Virūpākṣa's heart-place,
seize him firmly with ja and draw him;
from behind, the vajra holder pushes him.
Then, when he has become nondual with oneself,
inseparable,
set him to do whatever action is desired.
Virūpākṣa's retinues without exception
accomplish the actions of control.
To accomplish King Vaiśravaṇa:
oneself as the wrathful Amṛtakuṇḍalin,
with body color and hand emblems as before;
meditate thoroughly, clear and radiating light,
and recite the essence of one's own mantra a hundred thousand times.
When the samādhi can be transformed,
Vaiśravaṇa is thoroughly accomplished.
His form is the jewel beryl,
or else his color is blue and gleaming;
in his right hand he holds a jeweled staff,
in his left a vase of sense pleasures;
his head is beautified with various jewels.
bai śraṅ ṇa ye svā hā jaḥ hūṃ vaṃ hoḥ ku ber kaṅ kaṅ yaṅ yaṅ jaḥ hūṃ vaṃ hoḥ
Recite this a hundred thousand times, and so that it has life,
recite it again and again.
Oneself as Amṛta Heruka,
draw him with ja from Vaiśravaṇa's heart,
and bring him fully under control;
from behind, the vajra holder pushes him.
The wish-fulfilling jewel comes under one's control;
the yakṣa retinues are gathered like servants;
jewels are sent forth and the actions are accomplished;
he will perform whatever actions are desired.

This is the sixty-fifth chapter, from the Net of Illusion, on accomplishing the Great God and on arranging the maṇḍala of the Four Great Kings and accomplishing them.

Then the Blessed One, Great Delight, sang this song to the maṇḍala of the assembly in a most fearsome melody:

hūṃ Great fierce one, blazing like the fire at the end of time,
with the splendor of the rays of a hundred thousand suns,
a wrathful frown like a thousand flashes of lightning,
fangs bared, great devourer, ho!
hūṃ A fearsome roar that resounds like a thousand thunders,
a crash like a hundred thousand Mount Merus toppling,
the great prolonged laughter, a a ha la,
the great whirling of the scattering wind of the staff, ho!
hūṃ Wrathful wisdom, great light,
the wisdom maṇḍala clear everywhere,
blazing wisdom that conquers everywhere,
great drop of manifold wisdom, ho!
hūṃ King of wrath, great cloud,
wrathful maṇḍala that sends down a great rain,
treasure from which the maṇḍala arises as wished,
great drop of manifold wrath, ho!
hūṃ Great māra of all māras,
māra of māras, conqueror of māras,
terrifier even of the hosts of terror,
great terrifier, great drop, ho!
hūṃ Great vajra rock, the firm one,
vajra river, the gatherer,
vajra fire, great all-blazing one,
vajra wind, great scattering wind, ho!

Thus he sang it out.

This is the sixty-sixth chapter, on pleasing, from The Secret Essence, Ascertaining Reality.

Then the Blessed One, Great Delight, to the Tathāgata himself, the king of secret mantra.

Then the Tathāgata, from The Secret Essence, Ascertaining Reality, spoke this inspired utterance in order to teach the meaning of oneself and Vajrasattva gathering inseparably, again and again, into the authentic expanse, and of oneself seeing the face of Vajrasattva directly:

"Seeing the face as relative illusion:
all is in the manner of a mirage and an illusion.
All that appears and exists, without exception,
is known to be alike, in the manner of the moon in water,
without duality of object and thought.
Whoever knows it thus,
the expanse without ground or root, the momentary,
the expanse without before or after, suchness,
is the vajra that nothing can harm.
Vajrasattva abides in that.
Through conduct in harmony with the deity, that is seeing the face.
The authentic maṇḍala of intrinsic nature
is pure, joined in equality,
the dharmadhātu inseparable from oneself.
All is spontaneously present as awakening.
Whoever realizes it in this way
is one who has the two wisdoms,
and by that he sees the true essence.
That itself cannot be harmed by anything;
it is supremely firm and stable.
Luminous from the expanse of intrinsic nature,
it is truly called the supreme sattva.
If one realizes it thus, that is seeing the face;
likewise it is equality, perfection, spontaneous presence.
Meditate on the Tathāgatas and the five wisdoms,
the maṇḍala of the great self-arisen.
Through skill in means free from conceptualization itself,
if one meditates on the deity as clear and without substance,
in a manner devoid of seeing, hearing, and the rest,
if one meditates clearly on one's own wisdom,
anything at all is accomplished in what has no intrinsic nature,
yet in being accomplished there is no substance.
Just as the Blessed One
planted a palm tree in the midst of Māra's army,
and gathered all into the tree of awakening,
and the vicious ones, displeased by their very nature,
could never cut the palm tree down to wither,
that itself is Vajrasattva.
Seeing oneself and the samādhi,
that itself is seeing the vajra face.
Meditating on Vajrasattva himself,
if the samādhi is very familiar and clear,
all becomes the vajra itself,
mastering all, unharmed by anything.
Oneself and the sattva are inseparable,
just as things appear according to shared karma;
oneself and the sattva have one view and conduct:
that itself is seeing the vajra face.
The authentic maṇḍala of reflections,
great and coarse, is realized through skillful means.
Apprehending individual characteristics is the thought of wisdom;
the nondual connection is great skillful means.
All abides in the state of wisdom.
All that appears and abides, every bit of it,
because it has not moved from suchness,
is neither moving nor not moving;
abiding and not abiding cannot be divided.
All gathers into the expanse of equality.
Gathered, densely massed in that very place,
all is in such a state;
equality and duality are inseparable, unharmed by anyone.
That itself is the place where the sattva dwells.
The one of supreme family who realizes it thus,
being of the same family as the sattva himself:
that itself is seeing the vajra face.
The aggregates and elements, taken as the cause,
are one in suchness as their ground and root.
Appearing as illusion, in the manner of a reflection,
appearance itself is without substance;
that very substance displays variety.
The five aggregates themselves are the Tathāgatas;
the faculties and minds are the mind of awakening;
the objects and the times are the great ones;
the forms of the thought of self are the wrathful ones;
the four extremes of permanence and annihilation are the wrathful goddesses.
That mind is called self-awareness itself.
All turning into the expanse is Samantabhadra;
that itself is Vajrasattva.
Whoever realizes it thus,
because his intent accords with the sattva himself:
that itself is seeing the vajra face.
All phenomena without exception,
signified by names, phrases, letters,
and sounds, teach meaning;
yet all are like the letters of a single name.
None of them is a substance;
the nonsubstantial itself appears in variety.
In appearance itself there is nothing at all.
Though it performs all the various acts and agents,
it is without substance: that is the authentic itself.
That which nothing can harm
is not earth or stone, devoid of awareness;
it is utterly luminous.
Vajrasattva abides in this way.
Whoever realizes it thus
is the cause of the sublime supreme family;
because he is inseparable from the vajra family,
that itself is seeing the vajra face.
A person endowed with excellent force of intellect
realizes the meaning of inseparability from all.
Though the result has not yet become manifest,
because he has made his abode in the mind of awakening,
just as, for example, in the womb or in the egg,
though not manifest and obscured,
one emerges with great strength complete,
so as soon as the body of conceptual thought is cast off,
he gains mastery over everything
and comes to hold the sublime supreme family.
The supreme accomplishments equal to Vajrasattva
are completely perfected.
He also attains the realm of Sukhāvatī,
and comes to play in supreme awakening.
As the teacher of gods and humans,
having gained mastery over body, speech, and mind,
whatever he focuses on is accomplished.
He gains power over the four immeasurables,
and enjoys the supreme taste of bliss in all things.
Finally he attains the state in which the suffering
of birth, aging, sickness, death, and the rest is exhausted.
He comes to hold the supreme vajra family,
and through the blessing of great compassion
he leads all beings without exception
into the vajra family.
He cuts down the hosts of Māra and tames the afflictions;
he turns the wheel of Dharma perfectly;
and to all beings without exception
he also shows the way of impermanence.
To the śrāvakas, the way of the arhat;
to those of awakening, the way of the self-arisen;
the unsurpassed vehicle, the way of the buddhas;
to the spontaneously present, the vajra expanse.
He shows a way in harmony with each,
yet he does not move from the expanse of intrinsic nature;
while unmoving, he teaches everywhere.
All phenomena without exception
abide in such a state."

Thus it is, in vajra secret words. He said this to himself.

This is the sixty-seventh chapter, from The Secret Essence, Ascertaining Reality, on a single holy person of excellent force of intellect seeing the face of Vajrasattva.

Then the Tathāgata, in order to make the mantra holders' practice fruitful, condensed the root of the three hundred samayas from the King of Tantras, the Net of Illusion, and for the sake of the mantra holders spoke this inspired utterance:

In the worlds of the ten directions, numerous as atoms,
wrong conceptions, unbearable, are numerous as particles;
the remedies that tame such thoughts are inconceivable:
the Vinaya and vows beyond reckoning,
and all the many supreme vajra vehicles.
Though they move from that very nature and are free of elaboration,
through the deluded thought of self-awareness
views high and low are inconceivable;
to tame them, the teachings are given as is fitting.
If the authentic and wrong conception
abided by their essential nature,
there could be no altering or transforming them.
If they had an essence from the very beginning,
there would be no abandoning afflictions and accomplishing purification.
All without exception becomes of one taste.
Because the relative is like the moon in water,
it can become anything and be accomplished as anything.
For the yogin who realizes suchness,
ultimately there is no arising or ceasing,
and relative illusion accomplishes anything.
In times of degeneration, wrong conception itself,
if realized as free of apprehended and apprehender,
is the very nature of selfless wisdom.
Through compassion, skill in means, and samādhi,
anything is accomplished just as it is focused upon.
If you say each has a definite intrinsic nature,
then the authentic path itself could not be cultivated.
Therefore the authentic ones of great mind
explain all as without substance.
For example, one who knows the kinds of poison
appears unharmed by poison.
Even halahala poisons,
when processed, become rasāyana medicine,
turn into nectar, and bring about long life.
The afflictions, taught as individual and general:
by realizing their characteristics without error,
they become the path of purification.
Even the accomplishments of body, speech, and mind
are nowhere else; they are just that.
The five great inner ones too,
when their individual and general meaning is examined,
are completely pure, being without characteristics.
Because their individual energies are taken up,
they are emanated as great skillful means.
Because the signs do not abide by intrinsic nature,
they are joined into the expanse of wisdom.
What is called delusion
is a synonym for the name ignorance;
the two are explained as nondual, one in meaning.
Its individual characteristic is stated thus:
phenomena, self, and things,
imagined as having essential nature and so on:
thought itself becomes a stream of illusion,
the continuum of the elaborations of wrong conception.
Yet all is of one taste in the dharmadhātu:
the dharmatā of patience, the single eye.
Its general characteristic is stated thus:
impermanent, suffering, empty, and selfless.
Ignorance need not be abandoned;
awareness need not be accomplished;
all dissolves as one taste into the expanse.
Conceiving illusion as a thing:
the very thought of illusion is not observed;
that unobserved itself appears in variety.
In its very arising, it is the single dharmatā;
the expanse itself, unobserved, is suchness.
That itself is the dharmakāya, the great seal.
As for illusory things that are not observed,
even the thing itself as the object of thought
is dharmatā, unobserved, pure from the beginning.
Because illusion and the unobserved are inseparable,
and existence and nonexistence are inseparable,
if one gains mastery through samādhi,
that is the great seal of mind.
Dharmatā is not an object of reference;
while unobserved, it emanates as illusion;
the activities for those to be tamed are inconceivable:
that is the great seal of speech.
Thus delusion need not be abandoned;
it is the vast Vairocana.
Aversion, taught as individual and general:
the thought of utter malice
toward all illusory things
has the nature of wishing to destroy them.
Its general characteristic is stated thus:
impermanent, suffering, empty, and selfless.
In aversion and compassion there is nothing to abandon or accomplish,
because they are of one taste in the dharmadhātu.
The malicious one, by nature illusion,
if he realizes that all things perish,
then all illusory appearances
are not other than the dharmadhātu;
the unobserved itself arises as illusion.
The intrinsic nature of all phenomena
lacks any nature of self, life, or nourisher.
All appearances are the dharmadhātu;
malice and illusion are dharmatā.
All is unobserved: that is suchness.
All is inseparable: the seal of Dharma.
Unobserved illusion is utter malice;
thought is utter malice;
dharmatā and illusion are not observed.
Because dharmatā and illusion are not observed,
the ultimate and the relative are of one taste.
If one gains mastery through samādhi,
that is the great seal of mind.
The unobserved one of utter malice
performs inconceivable activities of taming:
that is the great seal of speech.

The general characteristic of aversion,
when thoroughly examined in its very truth,
is the characteristic of purification:
being nondual, aversion is not to be abandoned.

The characteristic of pride, explained,
is swaggering from within, puffed up with conceit;
it overpowers others.
The general characteristic, explained, is
impermanence, suffering, emptiness, and no-self.
There is no abandoning pride to achieve peace.
Because all is of one taste in the dharmadhātu,
relatively, as illusion, there is just this inner swagger.
It is not other than suchness:
unobserved dharmatā arises as swagger.
In the very arising of swagger is dharmatā;
the nature of swagger is unobserved and pure.
Because swagger and non-swagger are inseparable,
that is the great seal of the dharmakāya.
The unobserved itself swaggers as illusion,
and by swaggering it overpowers.
It is not other than the dharmadhātu:
because illusion and dharmatā are inseparable,
the ultimate and the relative are both one.
If one gains mastery through samādhi,
that is the great seal of mind.
Swaggering, unobserved, as illusion,
the taming of pride is inconceivable:
that is the great seal of speech.
The specific and general characteristics of pride
are the characteristic of purification.
Pride is not to be abandoned to achieve peace.

The characteristics of desire, specific and general:
it is clinging to all of existence.
The general characteristic, explained, is
impermanence, suffering, emptiness, and no-self.
There is no abandoning attachment to achieve non-attachment.
Because all is of one taste in the dharmadhātu,
relatively, in the nature of illusion,
desiring and being attached is illusory craving.
Because it is not other than the unobserved,
the unobserved itself arises as illusion.
Because phenomena are not other than oneself,
the unobserved itself arises as illusion.
Because conduct and illusion are of one taste,
neither dharmatā nor what bears it is observed.
Inseparable from all the Victors,
it is the seal of the dharmakāya.
Unobserved, one desires and clings as illusion;
desire and dharmatā are not observed;
because illusion and the unobserved are inseparable,
the two truths are inseparable and one.
If one gains mastery through samādhi,
that is the great seal of mind.
The unobserved expanse itself unfolding as desire
tames in inconceivable ways:
that is the great seal of speech.
The specific and general characteristics of desire
are the characteristic of purification.
Since attachment and non-attachment are not two,
the characteristic of desire is not to be abandoned.

The characteristic of jealousy in all its forms
is, toward the excellence of others,
a narrow-minded thought, an illusion of other,
scheming with stratagems, having the nature of illusion.
The general characteristic of jealousy, explained, is
impermanence, suffering, emptiness, and no-self.
There is no abandoning jealousy to achieve broad-mindedness.
Because all is of one taste in dharmatā,
toward the relative, illusory excellence of others,
the jealous thought is itself of the nature of illusion.
It is not other than the unobserved:
the unobserved itself arises as illusion.
The thought of means
to outdo that excellence is the dharmatā of illusion;
because it is inseparable from all the Victors of the three times,
that is the great seal of the dharmakāya.
Toward the unobserved, illusory excellence of others,
one holds jealous thoughts,
yet thought and jealousy are not observed;
because the two truths, in which illusion and the unobserved are inseparable,
are of one taste,
if one gains mastery through samādhi,
that is the great seal of mind.
Toward the unobserved excellence of others,
jealousy fully unfolding
performs inconceivable activities of taming:
that is the great seal of speech.
Jealousy, taught in its specific and general aspects,
is the characteristic of purification.
There is no abandoning narrowness to achieve breadth.

All the many kinds of sentient beings,
the five inner great elements and the rest, have been shown;
their specific and general characteristics are to be explained.
By not realizing skillful means and wisdom,
one is not liberated: that is the saṃsāra of the three realms.
If one sees with certainty in this way,
just as a disease fully held in check by mantra
cannot do harm to anyone,
so when skillful means and wisdom are realized,
the three poisons and suffering do no harm.
If one is not held by skill in means,
then even non-Buddhists (tīrthikas) who practice the virtues of the path
are born in the higher realms as gods and humans,
but do not attain the ground where there is no birth and death.
For one held by skillful means and compassion,
when all beings of the deeds of immediate retribution, without exception,
are killed out of a bodhisattva's compassion,
immeasurable merit is obtained.
If one does not kill them, one abandons those beings to their fate;
one is without compassion, and there is wrongdoing.
Desire, aversion, and delusion,
and great pride and jealousy:
since that awakening mind
is clearly the vajra Tathāgata,
the afflictions themselves have been clearly declared
to be the very essence of the Tathāgatas.
Because all phenomena are pure by nature,
saṃsāra becomes completely pure.
For one endowed with skillful means and wisdom,
all afflictions are causes of awakening.
Since the nature of saṃsāra is selfless,
therefore the Tathāgatas are inconceivable.
For one who has become endowed with skillful means and wisdom,
the distortions themselves are the path to awakening.
Because the nature of saṃsāra is unobserved,
nirvāṇa does not exist.
Through the means of the unsurpassed secret mantra,
things are accomplished just as they are taken up.
It is inconceivable and has nothing fixed.
Ultimately nothing whatsoever is established,
yet illusory emanations are inconceivable.
For one held by the great means,
even the path of the afflictions is liberation.
If one is not held by the great means,
even practicing virtue, one will not be liberated.
The yogin endowed with the ultimate,
even while engaging in the binding things of the afflictions,
becomes liberated.
Since neither saṃsāra nor nirvāṇa
exists at all,
when saṃsāra is fully understood,
that is the very limit of nirvāṇa.
If the afflictions and distortions,
even though held by skill in means,
did not become liberated and freed,
then all the deeds that bodhisattvas perform
for the sake of taming beings,
even appearing as hunters and courtesans, would be downfalls.
If the excellent conduct of bodhisattvas
were improper because ordinary people lack it,
then the supreme and most profound mantra
would come to be practiced by all people.
It is taught that amṛta is to be accepted.
If equal and unequal are inseparable,
one gains mastery of vajra body and speech;
therefore these are taught as substances of accomplishment.
If the equality of wisdom is not realized,
although some animals may possess a kind of dharma,
and there are the livelihood of sages, those who conform to permanence,
and those who renounce desire for as long as they live,
I have not declared that they are liberated.
Through the fourfold mode of the complete purity
of all phenomena, the meaning is directly realized.
Causes are pure by nature,
and results become completely pure.
The characteristic of the cause is pure:
it is the five outer great elements,
mind itself appearing as the duality of apprehended and apprehender.

The apprehended object itself is the great elements.
Since the continuum itself is one's own mind,
the great elements are pure from the level of the cause.
The complete purity of the result is this:
the bones of all sentient beings
are the vajra awakening mind;
flesh is the lotus and rakta;
excrement, urine, and saffron water
are pure because they dwell in each one's own body.
Through ultimate purity without objectification,
all phenomena, outer and inner,
are not the essence of anything at all;
being without arising and ceasing, they are pure from the beginning.
Relative purity from the beginning is this:
everything appears with the nature of illusion,
abiding unobserved, like the moon in water;
the unobserved itself appears in manifold ways,
equal from the beginning as the Tathāgata.
Therefore act without clean and unclean.
To reverse the conceptions of beings,
medicine should be accepted as foremost.
Thus sentient beings, being mistaken,
do not know all phenomena to be pure,
and do not realize the meaning of skillful means and wisdom.
Some eat for as long as they live;
some, like the sages,
give up meat and live on grass and water.
Though they eat, they accomplish nothing, not realizing the meaning.
Yogins endowed with skillful means,
because all is completely pure by nature,
realize it thoroughly through the expanse of equality,
and so, through the union of skillful means and wisdom,
they will accomplish it without doubt.
Conceiving of things and non-things is a sign;
conceiving of clean and unclean is a sign.
Abandoning even signs themselves,
through knowledge one will not remain in poverty.
These are rightly declared substances of accomplishment:
the awakening mind, the great flesh,
excrement, urine, and rakta.
The practitioner should apply himself to them diligently.
Great and wondrous accomplishments are attained:
mastery over life, the state of a vidyādhara,
flying through the sky, and unobstructedness are accomplished,
and finally, through the unborn vajra,
other things too, just as one aims at them,
will be accomplished: of this there is no doubt.

Through the very meaning of the secret essence,
do not abandon the chosen deity, but meditate constantly.
The primordial characteristic of the deity
is that of all who have gone in the three times.
If the deity is abandoned, practicing virtue becomes meaningless.
The meaning of not abandoning the deity is this:
the lord of the vajra body, speech, and mind
of all the maṇḍalas of the Sugatas of the three times,
not separate, nondual with the self
of all spontaneously present wisdom:
this is the meaning of meditating to completion.
If the Three Jewels are abandoned, the holy life becomes meaningless.
If one joins oneself in equality with the deity,
even with few qualities one will attain accomplishment.
Applying the equality that is there from the beginning,
meditate constantly as the deity itself.
One becomes equal to all the Sugatas,
and body, speech, and the five wisdoms are perfectly accomplished.

Do not disparage the vajra master.
He shows awakening, he is the guide,
like a guiding staff leading the blind;
he dries up the ocean of affliction and suffering.
To revile that master
is like putting out the lamp in a dark pit,
so that what is to be seen is not perceived:
one will manifestly undergo wretched suffering
for a long time, for many eons.
Therefore always be respectful toward him.
Hold him as far more important
than a king who has power over your body.
Honor him far above
the kind parents who gave you birth.

Vajra brothers and sisters,
who go to the realm of unsurpassed awakening,
are, through the power of dharmatā, samaya, and aspiration,
certain to reach the precious ground of awakening.
To turn against them and bear them grudges
is like striking oneself with a weapon:
for five hundred lifetimes,
one will cut off one's own life and be ruined.
In this life, various misfortunes
will come about as unhappiness,
and in the worlds beyond as well
one will experience, one after another,
the sufferings of the three lower realms and the unfree states,
and for a long time, never freed,
one will undergo extreme misery.
Practice together as though of one accomplishment.
Brothers and sisters follow along with nondual faith.
They are the unsurpassed maṇḍala of the Sugatas;
with clear wisdom, always cherish them.
Thus it was said.

Do not proclaim the secret to others.
The samayas of body, speech, and mind:
through the two kinds of yoga of equality,
having realized skillful means and wisdom in union,
one overcomes through the conduct of equality,
and realizes truly, unlike the ordinary.
Keep it utterly secret
from those on the side of non-realization, and from all people.
The various secret code words that were spoken
were made through skill in means.
It is not that the secret mantra has faults;
through great compassion and skill in means,
it is kept secret for the sake of unfit vessels,
the dull-witted, and those with evil karmic residue.
If by chance this secret meaning
is proclaimed to one with evil karmic residue,
then just as, when fire is kindled with a fire-drill,
the fire-drill itself is seen to burn,
and other things are burned as well,
so the excellent meaning of the Dharma is like fire.
Those of small fortune and inferior wisdom,
evil-minded and ignorant of cause and effect,
by denigrating equality
are hurled into the three lower realms.
If those who do not realize the secret meaning come to experience suffering,
then the one who spreads the secret, too,
will have a field ploughed on the tongue with which he spoke,
and will experience great suffering.
Therefore keep it secret for the sake of beings;
do not speak it even in the direction of the wind.
Do not break the continuity of mantra and mudrā.

What is proclaimed as mantra is this:
in the great maṇḍala of great equality,
where ultimate and relative are inseparable,
the syllable oṃ of body, speech, and mind,
and the rest, forty-two in all.
The characteristic of the great seal is this:
the body, speech, and mind and the five wisdoms
of the Sugatas of the ten directions and four times,
inseparable from oneself: that is the great seal.
Endowed with the qualities of clarity and diligence,
empowered as one wishes, without attachment,
one should make it familiar and stable.
Therefore, if there are interruptions and no diligence,
it is like wanting fruit
from a burnt seed:
the practice will be without result,
and one will remain in the unfree states of saṃsāra.
For one who has entered the great yoga,
all phenomena, outer and inner,
are without objectification, free of one and many,
neither edge nor center,
without essential nature and without characteristics.
Through the great seal of equality,
all the ten directions and four times,
and the qualities and faults of the transcendent and the mundane,
are without arising and ceasing,
established as no essence at all: they are equal.
Relative phenomena are without arising and ceasing;
unobserved, they are not the essence of anything.
Like the moon in water,
they appear in manifold ways and can perform activities.
The ten directions of the great seal,
all the three times and the three realms,
the mundane and the transcendent,
and all qualities and faults
abide from the beginning equal as illusion.
Having realized oneself as the king of mantra holders,
if one abides without wavering from
oneself as dharmatā, without arising and ceasing,
then all the great seals too,
completely, the body, speech, and mind of the Tathāgatas
of the ten directions and three times,
all inseparable, equal from the beginning,

unmixed, vast, and unbroken,
the celestial palaces and the maṇḍalas of sun and moon,
inconceivable and spontaneously present,
the inconceivable qualities of body, speech, and mind:
meditate on them as complete in the great expanse of equality.
Such are these three samayas;
if one abides in them, one will attain accomplishment.
With the limbs unimpaired, recite mantra,
and enjoy all the objects of desire.
If body, speech, and mind become stable,
one will attain boundless awakening.
If one transgresses the great samaya,
the danger is declared to be very great.
If, out of delusion, one transgresses
the buddhas and bodhisattvas
and the mantra conduct that accomplishes the aim,
that person's life will come to an end right there.
Guarding samaya, reciting mantra,
mudrā, and not breaking the continuity: these four
are to be held with great and steady diligence.
If the yogin's diligence becomes slight,
the danger of not attaining accomplishment is declared to be great.
Just as, if a man does not quickly
grasp the sword's edge with care,
his hand will surely be cut,
so the secret mantra practitioner without care
will die, or else will be without result.
Excellent yogins
keep the limbs of mantra unimpaired and clear,
make the ultimate unimpaired and pure,
generate great diligence, and accomplish swiftly.
Mantra and meditation unimpaired, mudrā clear:
ritual and implements were not taught as the main thing.
If they are complete, there is no fault;
if they are incomplete, there is no fault;
even if they are incomplete, one will attain accomplishment.
By joining oneself with one's own deity,
all accomplishments will be attained.
Even if the rite has too much or too little,
one will attain accomplishment; there is no fault.
Thus it is in secret words.
This is the sixty-eighth chapter, explaining in brief the fifteen roots of the three hundred samayas.

Then the Tathāgata Great Delight taught the dwelling place, the conduct, and the manner of gazing:
If you desire the meaning of the spontaneously present Great Vehicle,
vast, profound, and spacious, wisdom is swift.
Realizing the profound meaning, irreversible bliss;
without I and without self, spacious bliss.
As the dwelling place, the nondual dharmadhātu is shown:
great skillful means, the all-accomplishing, luminous love;
the all-delighting, fierce lady of the expanse;
a retinue of greater and lesser bodhisattvas;
a retinue of servants, led by the many classes of gods and spirits;
my own form with its marks, supremely adorned.
In the spacious center of the sky of the dharmadhātu,
wisdom swiftly goes to the meaning,
without going, without coming, without abiding:
the supreme awakening mind, ah, what bliss!
In the maṇḍala of inexhaustible wisdom,
wisdom soars as the play of compassion;
union in the nondual expanse, ah, what bliss!
Into the swamp of ignorance and delusion
the arrow of the lamp of wisdom is shot,
and the darkness of delusion is dispelled:
natural luminosity, the supreme of bliss.
Virtue and nonvirtue are not two;
thoughts and signs are not grasped as existent.
Since in what is naturally luminous from the beginning
there is neither virtue nor evil, what great bliss.
Since bliss and non-bliss are not two,
wisdom is unattached and completely pure.
All is inseparable wisdom;
mind itself being clear, ah, what bliss!
As adornment, all the maṇḍalas are beautiful,
formed from the cause that is wisdom;
in the clear state without obscuration, outside or within,
wisdom with a steady mind, what great bliss.
Toward everything endowed with the five enjoyments,
one holds no notion of clean and defiled;
since all is of one taste in dharmatā,
being nondual, what great bliss.
Through the samādhi of lesser and greater wisdom,
all is without error, in accord with the scriptural system;
saṃsāra is not abandoned, yet there is no suffering;
content in nonduality, ah, what bliss!
All without exception in the state of oneness:
the looking and the view, both are absent.
When the lamp of wisdom is held aloft,
all the realms of the ten directions are seen.
Because here and beyond are not two,
there is neither going nor anywhere to go.
Without leaping with great strides of the limbs,
in all the world systems of the ten directions,
the domain of the supreme Sage, the Teacher,
is accomplished just as one intends.
All phenomena are unobservable;
dharmatā, as illusion, is accomplished as anything at all.
When the meaning is examined with undefiled profundity,
there is nothing whatsoever that has not become pure.
Unfabricated wisdom, clear throughout,
is pure by nature.
Although it abides thus from the beginning,
the clear lamp is obscured
by the cataract of ignorance and delusion,
and one circles continually in the darkness of the three realms.
Through grasping at thoughts as signs,
one experiences bodies, enjoyments, and abodes,
and various pleasures and sufferings.
Who is it that makes this?
No truly mighty one made it;
one has merely bound oneself.
All phenomena without exception
have no core:
they are like an illusion, an optical illusion, an emanation,
a mirage, a rainbow, and the moon in water.
Whoever fully realizes it thus
is declared to be a buddha himself.
He dwells on the ground of a buddha;
he is unborn perfection,
and the supreme state of non-doing is complete as well.
Thus he spoke to himself.
This is the sixty-ninth chapter from the Net of Illusion, teaching the dwelling place and abode.

Then all the Tathāgatas spoke this practice for accomplishing the deathless vajra.

One who wishes to accomplish the great vajra corpse
should meditate on everything as being without birth and death,
as equality and as purity itself.
With the jaḥ hūṃ vaṃ of the wrathful Amṛtakuṇḍalin
one should summon.
With oneself and the wrathful one nondual,
one should practice again and again.
When the deity bodies have become clear,
one should place them beneath the great corpse.
Having arranged the entire maṇḍala there,
meditate on the maṇḍala of wrathful gods and goddesses.
Send forth messengers on errands to the ten directions,
and bring all without exception under control.
The practice should be meditated upon with one,
or else two, three, or five.
Having summoned the nṛ with many messengers,
place it in the corpse again and again.
It becomes long, yellow, and glossy in color.
Then, through the samādhi of amṛta,
for many hundreds of thousands of times,
recite in the threefold manner of recitation.
Reciting the vajra mantra, three-pointed,
the recitation issues from between them;
make it endowed with five rays of light.
When the corpse becomes limp and quivering,
rouse it with the five nectars.
Then set out the five syllables,
and meditate on them as clear and gleaming.
The five senses and the mode of the senses,
and on the five limbs the mode of the five families:
meditate on them with that vajra samādhi.
Then, when the corpse begins to move
and moves violently,
bind it very tightly with a noose,
and press down its limbs as well.
Show skull cups in the four directions.
Meditate on oneself in the form of Hayagrīva,
and recite the mantra many hundreds of thousands of times;
radiate to the ten directions and dissolve it into the corpse.
Then, when the corpse moves violently,
with that vajra samādhi
one should meditate on it as stable.
Then, when it flutters,
whatever is the a nṛ bri du,
strike it hard with the vajra and bring it down.
Having drawn the nṛ out from the mouth of the skull,
cut it with the teeth of vajra samādhi.
Eat it with the vajra mantra and dissolve it within;
one should meditate on it as nondual.
Then, when the corpse rises,
confer empowerment upon it from the vajra expanse,
pour amṛta water into it,
and set it to perform all activities.
The words the corpse then speaks are:
"Hero, what shall I do? Things of agelessness and birthlessness,
and all activities without exception, I shall do."
Through such a supreme kind of practice,
all ashes come back to life.
The places for the practice are charnel grounds,
fearsome mountain crags, the haunts of obstructors,
empty forests full of thorns,
places of slender willows encircled by mountains,
utterly secluded places,
and places where yakṣas and rākṣasas appear:
one should practice in such places.
One should practice for nine, twelve, thirteen,
or up to sixteen.
One will accomplish it without doubt.
The provisions for outer and inner sustenance,
the corpse of the five roads and the five medicines,
and all the grains should be kept at hand.
Always offer a great feast;
do not interrupt it, three or five times.
Eat your food from a skull cup,
and your drink likewise.
Keep a lamp burning constantly;
make its substance from the fat of the nṛ,
and likewise for anointing the limbs.
Make the wick from lotus material.
The vessel is a great conch without seams;
with or without locks of hair is acceptable.
If de byi pu tra is not available,
the color is found in one that is mottled.
To ward off obstacles to the vajra practice,
always seal the boundaries.
From the right and left legs of the nṛ,
take bones that are not thick but thin;
having made them into ki la ya,
recite with the kīla mantra and the wrathful mantra
a hundred thousand times.
On the palms and fingers of the right and left hands,
meditate on the ten wrathful gods and the ten wrathful goddesses
as blazing bodies of dhāraṇī.
Then, in the manner of nondual union,
the practitioner should meditate as the vajra.
Apart from the actual practice itself,
seal the boundaries in the ten directions as well.
Until accomplishment is attained,
practice the three, meditation, recitation, and mudrā,
heedfully, as if one's head were on fire,
without any interruption.
Through such a supreme kind of practice,
gods, mātṛkās, and rākṣasas serve like slaves;
the worldly Īśvara is brought under control;
the retinue and circumstances of the supreme vajra family
come about just as one wishes.
These great miraculous powers
should be displayed openly only once one is able to conceal one's body;
otherwise keep them utterly secret.
This was declared for the sake of the definitive accomplishment of the body.
This is the seventieth chapter, on the accomplishment of the vetāla.

From The Secret Essence, Ascertaining Reality, this definitive inspired utterance, called the samaya of dance and song, play and dance and song, was uttered:
In the maṇḍala where all
of the infinite ten directions and four times is inseparable,
in the celestial palace where sun and moon have risen,
having spread out seats of lotus without attachment,
on the ground of the palace of inexhaustible jewels,
in the blissful maṇḍala of awakening,
immeasurable in its greatness and smallness,
having fully arranged blissful awakening,
gathering all without exception, gathering into the expanse,
moving the body, stirring and shaking the limbs,
and all the gazing and conduct of the five senses
are declared to be the great seal.
The maṇḍala above, in the measure of sun and moon,
on the ground of the Unshakable,
having spread out seats of lotus without attachment,
in the manner of skillful means and wisdom without attachment,
all the limbs and senses, every one,
are completely pure by nature,
and are the samādhi of the five Tathāgatas.
Unbound from the beginning, entirely,
through the maṇḍala of superior samādhi,
on the head is the circle of the Tathāgata,
on the four limbs the circles of the four families.
Clearly visualizing colors, shapes, and mudrās,
and skillful means and wisdom,
radiate them to the ten directions and purify the lower realms;
gather them back into their places and radiate the maṇḍala.
All, both those who have passed beyond and those who have not,
are joined in the realm of the Sugata.
When the five great limbs are moved,
the maṇḍalas of the five families all move without exception;
the five classes of beings are set on the path of awakening,
and the five degenerations too are completely purified.
When good, beautiful, and fine garments are worn,
lights of many kinds radiate.
Then, endlessly, without limit,
one is adorned with the five wisdoms.
A person endowed with great skill of mind
should always meditate on the maṇḍala in this way:
not merely in words, but in the depths of the mind,
as inseparable from oneself.

Having become endowed with skillful means and wisdom,
facing toward the east,
one should leap with the great stride of a lion.
Meditating on traṃ, the syllable of the tongue,
emanate inconceivable hundreds of thousands of tongues,
and hri too, beyond conception.
Throughout the inconceivable ten directions and four times,
the maṇḍala of speech blazes with light.
The limbs of the maṇḍala of the one speech
are inconceivable and resound everywhere.
Sounds, names, and words are each distinct and clear.
Spreading out the faculty of the tongue, he speaks,
telling of both the liberated and the unliberated.
Yet in definitive truth alone,
in suchness, nothing at all was spoken.
In the maṇḍala of the faculty of the ear,
having clearly visualized the syllable traṃ,
the maṇḍalas of the ten directions and four times
are infinite and inconceivable;
by contemplating traṃ again, light radiates and blazes.
All the maṇḍalas of the directions and times without exception are heard;
the abodes of saṃsāra of the six classes of beings are heard as well;
sound and hearing are nondual.
Likewise smells, tastes, and tactile objects:
having clearly visualized their respective characteristics,
set out their seed syllables in the same way.
All the maṇḍalas of the buddhas without exception,
and all the six classes of beings without exception,
are enjoyed in the manner of non-attachment.
With smells and the rest, as supreme bliss,
all are truly offered to their satisfaction.
For all the six classes of beings without exception,
one also accomplishes whatever benefit is suited to them.
Through skill in means and compassion, one shows it thus.
Likewise, the Tathāgata family and the vajra family,
and the others, the Unattached and the rest, endowed with wisdom:
meditating on the five principal lords and five principal ladies
as endowed with skillful means and wisdom,
and meditating well on one cluster, or else three,
or a maṇḍala of five,
with skillful means and wisdom in mutual union,
make the deities clear, radiate light, and dissolve it back,
and meditate on the maṇḍala as blazing with rays of light.
When the samādhi has become very clear,
one should meditate on oneself and the ten directions
as inseparable and nondual.
Then, with skillful means and wisdom both,
view and conduct never parted,
when the maṇḍalas are clearly visualized,
facing east from the center,
move with the great stride of the feet,
move all the gestures of the hands,
and make the mudrās of dance and performance.
The maṇḍala radiating white light,
the bodhisattva completely pure,
utterly clear and blazing with light:
may that skillful means and wisdom be accomplished!
With the vajra that nothing can withstand,
the four māras are vanquished.
Vidyādhara of the vajra of the supreme vehicle:
may that skillful means and wisdom be accomplished!
Through oneself, the vajra of the family,
may there be no affliction or suffering!
Through mirror-like wisdom,
may skillful means and wisdom become clear!
Cutting off affliction and suffering at the root,
it dispels the darkness of ignorance.
Through the rising sun of the rays of compassion,
may skillful means and wisdom blaze with light!
Utterly solid, without birth and death,
displaying the play of vajra compassion,
compassion arises fully toward beings:
may skillful means and wisdom be endowed with love!

Having become endowed with skillful means and wisdom,
facing toward the direction of qualities,
moving the body, stirring and shaking the limbs,
one should also stamp with great strength.
The treasury from which all maṇḍalas without exception arise,
the inexhaustible precious jewel,
fulfilling the hopes of all without exception:
may that skillful means and wisdom be accomplished!
Showing the color of the center of the sky,
or else all of refined gold,
the inconceivable maṇḍala, inexhaustible:
may skillful means and wisdom be swiftly accomplished!
Though the precious qualities are inconceivable,
all abide in the state of equality:
such is the wisdom of equality.
May skillful means and wisdom become equal!
The great and wondrous qualities of the Sugata,
the realm of awakening, unattached from the beginning,
where all abides in reality itself:
may skillful means and wisdom be swiftly accomplished!

Having become endowed with skillful means and wisdom,
facing toward the west,
moving the first limb,
and making the dances very clear,
one should also stamp with great strength.
Though one enjoys all phenomena without exception,
like the lotus in the mud,
one abides in the manner of non-attachment:
may that skillful means and wisdom be accomplished!
Where karma and affliction have set,
there is not an atom of habitual tendency or sign:
may skillful means and wisdom be swiftly accomplished!
The unattached maṇḍala of awakening
is the expanse itself, come from nowhere;
it dwells in the expanse in the manner of non-abiding:
may skillful means and wisdom be swiftly accomplished!

Having become endowed with skillful means and wisdom,
facing toward the north,
moving the body, making the limbs dance,
with each of the mudrās made clear,
one should stamp with the great stride of the feet.
Through the wisdom of true realization,
having purified all the obscurations
of inconceivable eons,
may skillful means and wisdom be swiftly accomplished!
Through the blessing of body, speech, and mind,
the four māras are vanquished and adversity is dispelled;
without agent or action, all aims are accomplished:
may skillful means and wisdom be swiftly accomplished!
That which perfects all without exception
transforms into every kind of variety.

Displaying its color in the manner of green:
may skillful means and wisdom be swiftly accomplished!
Accomplishing all aims without exception,
perfecting all activities without exception,
completing the task with great diligence:
may skillful means and wisdom be swiftly accomplished!
Cutting off the net of suffering,
even while making every maṇḍala without exception,
coming from nowhere and going nowhere:
may skillful means and wisdom be swiftly accomplished!
All the sugatas without exception,
great in nature throughout all directions and times,
in the nonreferential maṇḍala of the mind
all gather without exception, utterly pure.
Through the genuine maṇḍala of great skillful means,
light radiates to all the sugatas.
The maṇḍala of bliss endowed with skillful means and wisdom
is gathered in by the skilled.
Of all things without exception, there is nothing
not included within the four families.
That itself is the very maṇḍala of the sugatas:
may skillful means and wisdom be blissfully accomplished!
Gathering the maṇḍala of the five families at the head,
rays of light of the five wisdoms radiate,
filling the ten directions with clarity:
may skillful means and wisdom blaze with light!
Likewise, the one who holds the supreme family
gathers all without exception into the expanse
and reveals all as Samantabhadra:
may skillful means and wisdom be accomplished as the principal!
Through the blessing of great compassion,
for all without exception who dwell in the directions and times,
the great wheel of Dharma is turned:
may skillful means and wisdom be supremely accomplished!
Because all beings without exception
possess the body of suchness,
to the deluded who do not see it, it is the color of the sky:
may skillful means and wisdom be accomplished in the expanse!
The body appears in every variety, as anything at all:
to those of great skillful means, in the form of equality;
to the śrāvakas, in the form of an arhat;
to the pratyekabuddhas, in the form of a rhinoceros;
